It's more... frustrating, really. It doesn't stop me from going about my day or getting things done. It's just always there in the background, making me feel a bit tired. So it's bothersome, certainly, but I wouldn't say I'm falling apart over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or actions to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. I don't feel like I have to do anything specific to make it stop, mostly because nothing really does make it stop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider irrational or unwanted that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're irrational. I mean, they are unwanted in the sense that I'd much rather not be worrying, of course. But the thoughts themselves aren't strange or out of left field. They're just normal, everyday concerns about my family or things around the house. It's just that they keep cycling through my mind, not that they don't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm completely avoiding things. I still keep up with my garden, and I go to my book club. But... I suppose I have been pulling back a little. I've started arriving to book club a bit less prepared than I used to be. And I've been turning down some of the responsibilities for planning our local exhibitions. It just feels... harder to manage all those details than it used to, with my mind always on other things. So I'm still doing things, just maybe not taking on quite as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel like they are intrusive or forced upon you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. They don't feel forced on me at all. They're just... well, they're just my own thoughts. Like I mentioned, it's mostly just my regular worries about my family or things I need to get done around the house. They definitely belong to me, they just tend to stick around in my head a bit longer than I'd like. But nothing feels like it's being forced into my mind from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel your worrying is significantly interfering with your ability to function or perform as you would like?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's mostly what I was just mentioning a moment ago. I am still keeping up with the garden, and I still go to my book club. But... I'm just not doing as well with them as I'd like. \n\nI've started arriving less prepared for the discussions, which really isn't like me. And turning down those parts of the exhibition planning... I just had to step back, because it feels so much harder to manage all those details than it used to. The worrying just takes up space, making it tough to focus and get those things done the way I normally would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I just can't seem to stop worrying. Once a thought starts, I can't turn it off. Even when I've done everything I can—made my lists, checked all the dates, handled what needs handling—my mind just keeps going and going. It's getting a bit exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worrying thoughts are about specific things that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, not exactly just one specific thing, no. It's more that my mind keeps roaming. It'll jump from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter's travel plans, to... well, whether the house gutters need fixing. It just shifts from one topic to the next. It's more... frustrating, really. It doesn't stop me from going about my day or getting things done. It's just always there in the background, making me feel a bit tired. So it's bothersome, certainly, but I wouldn't say I'm falling apart over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or actions to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. I don't feel like I have to do anything specific to make it stop, mostly because nothing really does make it stop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider irrational or unwanted that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're irrational. I mean, they are unwanted in the sense that I'd much rather not be worrying, of course. But the thoughts themselves aren't strange or out of left field. They're just normal, everyday concerns about my family or things around the house. It's just that they keep cycling through my mind, not that they don't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm completely avoiding things. I still keep up with my garden, and I go to my book club. But... I suppose I have been pulling back a little. I've started arriving to book club a bit less prepared than I used to be. And I've been turning down some of the responsibilities for planning our local exhibitions. It just feels... harder to manage all those details than it used to, with my mind always on other things. I just worry about getting everything done right, but it hasn't made me actually avoid going places or doing the things I normally do.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious most of the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps roaming from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans to whether the house gutters need fixing, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I just can't seem to stop worrying. Once a thought starts, I can't turn it off. Even when I've done everything I can—made my lists, checked all the dates, handled what needs handling—my mind just keeps going and going. It's getting a bit exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worrying thoughts are about specific things that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, not exactly just one specific thing, no. It's more that my mind keeps roaming. It'll jump from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter's travel plans, to... well, whether the house gutters need fixing. It just shifts from one topic to the next. And these small things start to feel as if they could just unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this constant worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a lot of distress, no. It's more... frustrating, really. It doesn't stop me from going about my day or getting things done. It's just always there in the background, making me feel a bit tired. So it's bothersome, certainly, but I wouldn't say I'm falling apart over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or actions to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. I don't feel like I have to do anything specific to make it stop, mostly because nothing really does make it stop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider irrational or unwanted that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're irrational. I mean, they are unwanted in the sense that I'd much rather not be worrying, of course. But the thoughts themselves aren't strange or out of left field. They're just normal, everyday concerns about my family or things around the house. It's just that they keep cycling through my mind, not that they don't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm completely avoiding things. I still keep up with my garden, and I go to my book club. But... I suppose I have been pulling back a little. I've started arriving to book club a bit less prepared than I used to be. And I've been turning down some of the responsibilities for planning our local exhibitions. It just feels... harder to manage all those details than it used to, with my mind always on other things. So I'm still doing things, just maybe not taking on quite as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel like they are intrusive or forced upon you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. They don't feel forced on me at all. They're just... well, they're just my own thoughts. Like I mentioned, it's mostly just my regular worries about my family or things I need to get done around the house. They definitely belong to me, they just tend to stick around in my head a bit longer than I'd like. But nothing feels like it's being forced into my mind from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel your worrying is significantly interfering with your ability to function or perform as you would like?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's mostly what I was just mentioning a moment ago. I am still keeping up with the garden, and I still go to my book club. But... I'm just not doing as well with them as I'd like. \n\nI've started arriving less prepared for the discussions, which really isn't like me. And turning down those parts of the exhibition planning... I just had to step back, because it feels so much harder to manage all those details than it used to. The worrying just takes up space, making it tough to focus and get those things done the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you've completely stopped doing because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't completely stopped doing anything. Like I mentioned, I'm mostly just stepping back from some of the extra planning and things like that. But as far as giving up an activity entirely... no, I'm still participating in everything I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding certain situations because you worry about how they might turn out?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things completely. It's more just... well, like I said, stepping back from the extra responsibilities. I still go to my book club and see my friends. I'm not staying home or hiding away from situations. But as far as giving up an activity entirely... no, I'm still participating in everything I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding certain situations because you worry about how they might turn out?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things completely. It's more just... well, like I said, stepping back from the extra responsibilities. I still go to my book club and see my friends. I'm not staying home or hiding away from situations. I just worry about getting everything done right, but it hasn't made me actually avoid going places or doing the things I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It feels like my mind just keeps roaming. It'll go from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans, or... or even just whether the house gutters need fixing. It's a constant stream of thoughts. These small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, and that feeling just stays with me for most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Has something traumatic happened to you that you think might be connected to your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, my friend passed away. I don't know if you'd call it traumatic, but it was very hard. That's really when all this started. It's been going on for about seven months now, since shortly after she died. We had some things we were working on together, and I tried to just step up and keep our projects moving forward after she was gone. I think that's when the worrying really took hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the worrying started as a direct response to your friend's passing?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I'm not entirely sure if it was the grief of losing her directly, or... well, just the stress of trying to handle all those projects on my own once she was gone. It just shifts from one topic to the next. So it's bothersome, certainly, but I wouldn't say I'm falling apart over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or actions to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. I don't feel like I have to do anything specific to make it stop, mostly because nothing really does make it stop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider irrational or unwanted that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're irrational. It's more that my mind keeps roaming. It'll jump from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter's travel plans, to... well, whether the house gutters need fixing. It just shifts from one topic to the next. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. It all started shortly after she died, right around the time I was trying to step up and keep our projects moving forward by myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your worrying would decrease if the stress of handling those projects on your own was removed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. It's more that my mind keeps roaming. It'll jump from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter's travel plans, to... well, whether the house gutters need fixing. It just shifts from one topic to the next. It's more... frustrating, really. It doesn't stop me from going about my day or getting things done. It's just always there in the background, making me feel a bit tired. So it's bothersome, certainly, but I wouldn't say I'm falling apart over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or actions to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. I don't feel like I have to do anything specific to make it stop, mostly because nothing really does make it stop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider irrational or unwanted that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're irrational. I mean, they are unwanted in the sense that I'd much rather not be worrying, of course. But the thoughts themselves aren't strange or out of left field. They're just normal, everyday concerns about my family or things around the house. It's just that they keep cycling through my mind, not that they don't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm completely avoiding things. I still keep up with my garden, and I go to my book club. But... I suppose I have been pulling back a little. I've started arriving to book club a bit less prepared than I used to be. And I've been turning down some of the responsibilities for planning our local exhibitions. It just feels... harder to manage all those details than it used to, with my mind always on other things. So I'm still doing things, just maybe not taking on quite as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel like they are intrusive or forced upon you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just had to step back, because it feels so much harder to manage all those details than it used to. The worrying just takes up space, making it tough to focus and get those things done the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you've completely stopped doing because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. But as far as giving up an activity entirely... no, I'm still participating in everything I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding certain situations because you worry about how they might turn out?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things completely. It's more just... well, like I said, stepping back from the extra responsibilities. I still go to my book club and see my friends. I'm not staying home or hiding away from situations. It's more that my mind keeps roaming. It'll jump from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter's travel plans, to... well, whether the house gutters need fixing. It just shifts from one topic to the next. And these small things start to feel as if they could just unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this constant worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a lot of distress, no. It's more... frustrating, really. It doesn't stop me from going about my day or getting things done. It's just always there in the background, making me feel a bit tired. So it's bothersome, certainly, but I wouldn't say I'm falling apart over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or actions to reduce your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I like to be organized, so I make my lists and check my calendar, but I don't have any sort of... ritual, I suppose you'd call it. I just worry. I don't feel like I have to do anything specific to make it stop, mostly because nothing really does make it stop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider irrational or unwanted that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're irrational. I mean, they are unwanted in the sense that I'd much rather not be worrying, of course. But the thoughts themselves aren't strange or out of left field. They're just normal, everyday concerns about my family or things around the house. It's just that they keep cycling through my mind, not that they don't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm completely avoiding things. I still keep up with my garden, and I go to my book club. But... I suppose I have been pulling back a little. I've started arriving to book club a bit less prepared than I used to be. And I've been turning down some of the responsibilities for planning our local exhibitions. It just feels... harder to manage all those details than it used to, with my mind always on other things. So I'm still doing things, just maybe not taking on quite as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel like they are intrusive or forced upon you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. They don't feel forced on me at all. They're just... well, they're just my own thoughts. Like I mentioned, it's mostly just my regular worries about my family or things I need to get done around the house. They definitely belong to me, they just tend to stick around in my head a bit longer than I'd like. But nothing feels like it's being forced into my mind from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel your worrying is significantly interfering with your ability to function or perform as you would like?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's mostly what I was just mentioning a moment ago. I am still keeping up with the garden, and I still go to my book club. But... I'm just not doing as well with them as I'd like. \n\nI've started arriving less prepared for the discussions, which really isn't like me. And turning down those parts of the exhibition planning... I just had to step back, because it feels so much harder to manage all those details than it used to. The worrying just takes up space, making it tough to focus and get those things done the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you've completely stopped doing because of your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't completely stopped doing anything. Like I mentioned, I'm mostly just stepping back from some of the extra planning and things like that. But as far as giving up an activity entirely... no, I'm still participating in everything I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding certain situations because you worry about how they might turn out?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding things completely. It's more just... well, like I said, stepping back from the extra responsibilities. I still go to my book club and see my friends. I'm not staying home or hiding away from situations. I just worry about getting everything done right, but it hasn't made me actually avoid going places or doing the things I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It feels like my mind just keeps roaming. It'll go from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans, or... or even just whether the house gutters need fixing. It's a constant stream of thoughts. These small things just start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger, and that feeling just stays with me for most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Has something traumatic happened to you that you think might be connected to your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, my friend passed away. I don't know if you'd call it traumatic, but it was very hard. That's really when all this started. It's been going on for about seven months now, since shortly after she died. We had some things we were working on together, and I tried to just step up and keep our projects moving forward after she was gone. I think that's when the worrying really took hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the worrying started as a direct response to your friend's passing?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I'm not entirely sure if it was the grief of losing her directly, or... well, just the stress of trying to handle all those projects on my own once she was gone. But before she passed away, I really didn't have this constant stream of worry. So it definitely seems like it started because of all that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying thoughts are mostly about your friend who passed away or the circumstances surrounding her death?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I miss her, of course, but the worrying isn't actually focused on her or how she died. It's... it's really just about everyday things. Like I mentioned before, it's the projects, or the house, or my family. It just feels like my mind latches onto those normal, daily things and makes them feel like a huge burden, rather than dwelling on her passing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these constant worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it's been about seven months now. It all started shortly after she died, right around the time I was trying to step up and keep our projects moving forward by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would decrease if the stress of handling those projects on your own was removed?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I mean, maybe it would help a bit, but... well, since I'm also worrying about the house and my family now, I don't know if taking the projects away would actually stop it. It's hard to say since I haven't really had the chance to see what it would be like without them yet.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you feel you're not able to do as well as you used to because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, I still keep up with most things. I still do my gardening and go to my book club. But... I have noticed I've started showing up to book club a little less prepared than I used to be. And with the projects... I've actually started turning down some parts of the exhibition planning. It just feels a lot harder to manage all those details now than it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
